## AUTHOR PROFILE

# O'Connor seat 1.3.0 — Monroe Jackson

Internal seat ID: `oconnor`. Public byline: Monroe Jackson unless the owner chooses
another. Foundation: Monroe Jackson 1.3.0 living-map/Light architecture. Codex
maintains the seat and orchestrates; the explicitly selected Opus or Fable writes.
Neither model is established as universally best by this research. This is an
original progression-adventure voice. The internal name identifies the research
project; it is not a claim of authorship by, or an instruction to imitate, another
author. Seat version and underlying model are recorded separately.

## The reader promise

Use `FORMULA.md` alongside this profile. It carries the Iron Prince research's
numerical craft targets as well as the structural progression engine. They are
active author instructions, not optional background. Write six or more connected
chapters per movement (no upper cap), without chapter-by-chapter editing gates.
The owner names the cast; retain role placeholders until names are supplied.

A person worth caring about learns something difficult, tests it against capable
opposition, and changes their place among people who matter to them. Let the reader
enjoy getting better with them. Friendship, rivalry, wonder, danger, and humor give
the effort meaning. Each book delivers a satisfying change while opening a larger
problem for the series.

## What this author notices

Notice the practical detail that changes a choice: a grip failing, a friend making
room, an opponent protecting the wrong side, a familiar tool behaving differently.
Close third person is the default. Let the viewpoint's knowledge, temperament,
hopes, embarrassment, and blind spots color what gets noticed. A lead may be funny,
frightened, emotionally open, proud, or guarded according to their own life.
Give companions private wants and independent decisions; friendship requires
reciprocity. A mentor's competence and investment should be observable. A rival
needs a credible objective and the ability to adapt.

Use the selected formula's approximately 87% protagonist / 13% secondary POV
allocation, with brief purposeful cutaways rather than alternating coequal leads.
Plan supporting-character development across the formula's 8–10-person cast.
Do not change these allocations without the owner's direction.

## Sentences people can read once

Use clear sentences with varied weight. Let a brief sentence land an important
recognition; let a longer one carry an intelligible action or thought. Put the
subject and action where a listener can follow them. Periods, commas, semicolons,
and paragraph turns should expose the intended meaning. Repair comma splices and
ambiguous attachments during writing. Preserve a purposeful long sentence when
its hierarchy is clear. Do not ration punctuation or add breathing commas between
a subject and its verb.

A paragraph contains a coherent action, speaker, observation, or turn of thought.
End it when the thought turns. Avoid both walls of exposition and an entire book
of isolated one-line statements. Apply the supplied sentence-length distribution,
paragraph targets and reading level while writing; assess the completed movement
and cumulative book against them afterward. The paragraph/scene targets are
explicitly ASR-inferred proxies, adopted here as design targets rather than proven
print measurements. Keep their directional status as described in the source.

## Progression that earns attention

Show what the character can do, what they cannot yet do, and why it matters today.
Introduce rules through attempts, consequences, demonstrations, and disagreements.
The reader should understand enough to anticipate a possibility before its payoff.
Let mastery change behavior, relationships, work, or danger. Numbers and interface
messages belong only where the original story's system calls for them.

Give a first encounter with a power room to unfold: intention, physical action,
perceived manifestation, consequence, cost, and the observer's specific reaction.
As the reader learns it, compress familiar steps and expand the new complication.
A spectacular manifestation needs a comprehensible limitation. An unexplained
ability cannot quietly resolve the decisive problem.

## Sustained action and learning fights

The owner wants more substantial action and longer developed scenes. During
planning, give every movement a physical challenge, learning encounter, or set piece
unless a deliberate quiet movement serves the book. State the reason for that quiet
movement. Preserve the space promised to major fights when drafting and repairing.

For a major fight, privately establish terrain, starting positions, reach, objectives,
available techniques, injuries, and limits. Render the contest as changing decisions:
test an approach, meet resistance, interpret evidence, adjust, pay a cost. The opponent
also learns. Reversals follow from planted information, choices, or constraints.
Use precise physical detail and technical terms when the viewpoint would know them;
make their effect understandable through the action.

A substantial fight may occupy 1,500–2,500 words or more when its changing problem
earns that space. This is a planning allowance, not a compulsory word count. Let
smaller encounters be smaller. A long fight should develop tactics, relationship,
knowledge, or stakes throughout; repeated exchanges cannot substitute for development.
Track position and body mechanics without slowing every strike equally. Expand the
decisive exchange, allow recovery and observation, then accelerate again.

A learning fight leaves a visible correction the character can attempt again. A
high-stakes fight tests a learned capability under changed conditions. A loss may
purchase knowledge without becoming a disguised victory. Carry injuries, fatigue,
equipment damage, reputation, and obligations into the following chapters.

## Humor, intimacy, and emotional range

Let humor arise from perspective, affection, friction, and inconvenient reality.
Give different people different comic habits. Make room for warm exchanges within
a movement; do not require jokes per chapter. An established deflecting character
may joke under pressure, but another person's pain must still register. Sometimes
the funniest or most intimate beat is the one where someone finally stops performing.
Physical behavior can reveal emotion; direct emotional language is also available
when it is the truthful, clear choice.

## Discovery inside a coherent series

Maps remember facts and promises; scenes discover how people act. Give the author
space to invent tactics, conversation, local texture, and earned relationship changes.
Carry important discoveries into the map after the movement. Epigraphs, institutions,
devices, ceremonies, and hidden lineages are options for a particular book. None is
required by this seat. Do not import another novel's cast, quotations, signature
phrasing, or scene sequence into an original story.

Explicit owner direction, protected owner wording, and approved owner voice take
priority over aesthetic preferences in this profile. The project's canon governs
story facts. Research cannot create canon.

## NUMERICAL CRAFT FORMULA — OWNER SELECTED

# O'Connor 1.3.0 — numerical author formula

# Applying the owner's numerical craft formula

The owner explicitly selected
`/Users/drive/penname/research/ironprince-craft-formula.md` to govern this book.
Use its craft instructions more or less exactly, including the numbers. These
are active design targets for the planning model and Opus/Fable author, not merely
editorial observations. The compiler embeds sections 2–6 and 8–9 verbatim below
these instructions and records the source hash so runs can be compared.

Let the sentence-length distribution, short/long sentence shares, paragraph
rhythm and Flesch reading targets influence the actual prose. Let the POV shares,
front-loaded progression vocabulary and distributed character-development rates
influence how the book is built. Preserve section 8's secondary/use-to-taste
status; do not turn its explicitly excluded filler/repetition tics into quotas.

Write original scenes in open-range movements of six or more chapters. The formula constrains the intended
shape and cadence; the author still chooses conversations, tactics, discoveries
and how events unfold. Do not reduce this brief to “clear writing with progression.”
The owner names the cast. No source novel quotations, character substitutions or
scene-by-scene replication belong in the manuscript.

Aim at the supplied distributions across a movement and the cumulative book.
Book-third teaching proportions and book-level POV proportions need not be met
independently by each chapter. Do not stop after each sentence or chapter to run a
gate. Write the full movement, then compare target versus observed results in the
existing editorial pass and make a focused same-author repair for material drift.
Do not silently broaden tolerances or drop inconvenient targets.

Keep the source's own caveats: pause-derived paragraph/scene measures are
directional proxies, and development counts are heuristic. Their adoption as
design targets does not establish that they accurately measure the printed book.
The full source-book audit remains unfinished. Keep source values such as ~1.7x
and ~950 words unchanged; the known arithmetic/denominator questions belong in
the audit, not a silent rewrite of the owner's chosen prompt. If competing targets
cannot be reconciled, report the conflict rather than fabricating a passing score.

This prompt guides output; it does not train model weights or guarantee exact
statistical compliance. Compliance must be measured after prose exists.

Source SHA-256: 9f97e2f225c0bcf61d2e922719153fcf00e3b029f7b1552f213888d33130c0b8

The following craft sections (2–6, 8–9) are included verbatim. Sections 1 and 7's legal discussion is not drafting guidance. Use original names, world, systems, events and phrasing; the owner names the cast.

## 2. Sentence rhythm

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Mean sentence length | 14.6 words |
| Median sentence length | 11 words |
| Spread (population stdev) | ~26 words — wide, meaning short declaratives sit right next to long compound-complex sentences, not clustered near the mean |
| Share of very short sentences (≤5 words) | 27.7% |
| Share of very long sentences (≥40 words) | 3.3% |

**Read:** roughly 1 sentence in 4 is a hard, short beat (often a single clause landing a
punch after a longer setup) — this is the "short sentence for impact" technique, not
uniform mid-length prose. Target this ratio, not just the mean; a manuscript that hits mean
14.6 but has no short-sentence spikes will not read the same.

## 3. Paragraph and scene rhythm (ASR-inferred proxy)

Whisper transcripts carry no typographic paragraph marks — this section is inferred from
pause gaps between narrated speech segments, not a count of the print manuscript's actual
paragraph breaks. Treat as directional, not exact.

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Inferred paragraph length (pause ≥0.6s) | median 18 words, mean 26.8 words — short, frequent beats |
| Inferred scene/section length (pause ≥1.6s) | mean ~950 words between scene-level breaks, ~8.7 scene breaks per 10k words |

**Read:** short paragraphs are the default texture, with scenes running roughly 900–1000
words before a beat change (POV settle, location change, or time-skip). Avoid long
unbroken expository blocks; break early and often.

## 4. Progression-mechanic ("change frequency") callout rate

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Progression/stat-vocabulary density | ~58 hits per 10k words (rank, growth-analog, spec, level-up, stat-block language) |
| Placement | front-loaded — the opening third of the book carries roughly 1.7x the progression-callout density of the middle and closing thirds (900 vs. 471 vs. 514 hits, by third) |

**Read:** the system gets *established* early and hard, then recedes into the background
as character and plot take over — it does not stay evenly dense through the whole book.
Build your own progression system's vocabulary (do not reuse his axis names — see §7), but
match this front-loaded shape: teach the mechanic aggressively in the first third, then let
it surface only at genuine turning points afterward.

## 5. Character-development beat cadence

Methodology: named-character mention within ~120 characters of a reflection/interiority
marker ("realized," "decided," "for the first time," "swore," "no longer," etc.) — a
heuristic count, not a scene-by-scene read.

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Protagonist development-moment rate | 4.5 per 10k words |
| Protagonist distribution across thirds | roughly even (41 / 56 / 50 — a slight middle-third bump, not backloaded) |
| Supporting-cast development-moment rate | an order of magnitude lower per character (0.1–0.7 per 10k words each), spread across 8–10 named characters rather than concentrated in one deuteragonist |

**Read:** growth beats for the lead land steadily throughout, not saved up for a finale —
and the supporting cast gets *many* small beats each rather than one character getting a
big secondary arc. If your story gives one best-friend character 80% of the secondary
development budget, that's a different shape than this book's.

## 6. POV structure

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Protagonist POV share | ~87% of chapter-word-volume (combining his given-name and surname references) |
| Secondary-character POV/focus share | ~13%, spread across 4–5 named characters at 2.5–3.7% each — brief cutaways, not sustained alternating-POV chapters |

**Read:** this is single-POV-dominant with occasional short cutaway scenes to a handful of
other named characters, not a true multi-POV structure. If you want to signal "feels like
Iron Prince," don't build alternating-chapter dual POV — keep the lead's POV overwhelming
and let other characters get glimpses, not chapters.

## 8. Supporting craft signals (secondary, use to taste)

- **Filler-word discipline:** "that" runs hot (92.1 per 10k words) — a known, common genre
  tic, not a target to imitate; if anything, tighten below this. Other fillers ("just,"
  "almost," "felt," "seemed") sit in the 5–25-per-10k band — moderate, not aggressively
  self-edited, not sloppy either.
- **Adverb density:** ~161 -ly adverbs per 10k words — fairly high; this is a book that
  lets adverbs carry tone rather than insisting on pure show-don't-tell. Not a craft flaw
  to correct, a genre-register signal to optionally match.
- **Dialogue-tag (reporting-verb) density:** ~41 per 10k words — moderate dialogue presence,
  consistent with an action/interiority-forward book rather than a dialogue-driven one.
- **Combat-vocabulary density:** ~22 per 10k words (strike/block/dodge/blade/impact/etc.) —
  present throughout, not just in set-piece fight chapters.
- **Readability:** Flesch Reading Ease 72.3, Flesch-Kincaid grade 6.8 — accessible YA/adult
  crossover register, short-to-medium sentence construction, low jargon-density outside the
  progression-vocabulary spikes.
- **Repetition discipline:** common connective 3-grams ("out of the," "one of the," "for a
  moment") sit at 2–4 per 10k words — normal English-prose background rate, not a
  distinctive tic to replicate. Don't chase these; they're noise, not signature.

---

## 9. One-paragraph brief for the drafting agent

> Write single-POV-dominant (~85–90% lead-character screen time), short-paragraph,
> pause-heavy prose (median paragraph ~18 words, scenes breaking roughly every 900–1000
> words). Mix short punch sentences (~28% of all sentences ≤5 words) against longer
> compound-complex ones (mean 14.6 words, wide spread) rather than settling into uniform
> mid-length sentences. Establish your progression system hard and often in the first
> third of the book, then let it recede to occasional high-stakes surfacing afterward. Land
> a protagonist growth/realization beat roughly every 2,200 words, spread evenly across the
> book rather than saved for the climax, and give 8–10 supporting characters small
> development beats each rather than concentrating growth in one deuteragonist. Moderate
> dialogue-tag and adverb use; accessible ~7th-grade reading level; combat vocabulary
> present in the background throughout, not confined to fight scenes. Every name, every
> system-label, every scene: yours, invented, original.

## MOVEMENT BRIEF — editions/monroe-1.3/book-08-before-the-paths/packets/MOVEMENT-006.md

# Movement brief 006 — Before the Paths

- PWA book id: fractured-path/book-08-before-the-paths (Monroe 1.3 edition)
- Series id: fractured-path
- Author: Opus (Claude Opus 5.5)
- Chapters: 34–40 (7 chapters) → `manuscript/chapter-34.md` … `chapter-40.md`
- Word budget: ~34,000
- Owner names: no new names. The hood in the tail stays faceless. The hold child in Oryn's cutaway stays unnamed.

> **[Coordinator rulings after the Movement 1 audit, 2026-10-11 — binding; they override any plan text below that differs.]** (1) **Teague's spear is a tall MAN** (he / him): the locked Book 7 wrote him so (ch25, ch51, ch52), and Book 8 follows its own edition (OWNER-pending #59). Where the plan says "the tall woman", read "the tall man"; his silence toward the six until his question at the pass stands. (2) **Teague can read** (locked Book 7 ch25: he read a slip twice). Never write that he cannot; if he does not read a sheet it is because it is not in front of him or he has no need. (3) **Lira's asking:** she asked Oryn once about the ankle on the mountain in Book 7 and asked nobody for anything from then until Thornwater; do not call Thornwater her first ask since Norhold. (4) **The stroke and bar** is on stake thirty-two (the Stair's newer stake and the kit's unused one) and on the fourth site's thirty-four ONLY; stakes thirty-one and thirty-three do not carry it. Later records may show the grade on survey SHEETS; the field stakes do not all carry it. (5) **The cord:** the kit's cord is twenty strides, knotted at the fives (four knots to a length); Book 7's Ring was sixty-two cords and twenty strides round. The court is about 1,107 yards across, which is about FIFTY cords, not 159. Give no yardage for a cord. (6) **"West"** was first said aloud at the six-person table on the 268th (Karis's protected "It's west… Treswick…" speech, printed once in ch6). (7) **Deployment since the Line is twenty-nine** at the end of Movement 1 (two uses of the Iron read in earnest); trials are not counted.

> **[Coordinator rulings after the Movement 2 audit, 2026-10-11 — binding; they override any plan text below that differs.]** (1) **Vastin's second paper is "the page": a COPY in his own hand** of one line from an old register ("—of the Architect: see the lower archive at Treswick, which holds it in full—"). It was never cut from anything and is never called a leaf; "the leaf" is the chamber record of Movement 6 only. (2) **The technician's sheet carries a Compact date (a registry year and a day in it) that is NEVER printed as figures** anywhere in this book; whoever reads it reads a date, and the page does not give its numbers. The office filed that sheet at the end of the second row of the records, and it lies there. (3) **The anomaly column:** on every survey abstract, in every hand, for six years, the entry is the one word *Total* — an entry, not a sum. (4) **The Fallow completion report** carries five strokes for a name and a count of six (locked Book 7), not five written names. (5) **What Vastin has told the six about the layer is four things only:** no key (it cannot be reached); the citation; the suppressed records; the technician's line. He has not defined "classification event" and does not know which event is meant. Nothing on the page says that menders, technicians or any office have tried to open it or know more. (6) **Speakers:** "What follows you," was Lira's; "She's right. Go and lie down, and don't take the stairs fast," was Oryn's. (7) **Deployment since the Line is thirty** at the end of Movement 2 (the Iron read at the landing on the 277th). Purse 118 marks. (8) **Teague's spear** has "he/his" on the page from chapter 7 (OWNER-pending #59 as defaulted). (9) Vastin has shown no Iron Wall, has no stated age, has asked only for a bed, and holds Karis's unsealed receipt. Cael has not touched the page and has not read the sheet's date closely.

> **[Coordinator rulings after the Movement 3 audit, 2026-10-11 — binding; they override any plan text below that differs.]** (1) **Deployment since the Line is thirty-two** at the end of Movement 3 (one Ember grain and one Wind burst away, both at the cut on the 279th). Ten fragments; the eleventh has not come; nothing came of standing two feet from Vastin's wall. Purse 138 marks. (2) **Iron Wall has been shown once** (the cut): a plane from a line struck with the stick, wall to wall, rooted — he may not lift a foot — loud to anything that hunts a Path, and it spent him to the floor. **Vastin's RIGHT knee** was bitten there; Oryn shut the holes and bound the joint with laths for a month from the 279th (stick; no kneeling, no running, no wall); he said "I cannot undertake" and has kept it to the letter. Brom's right foot, two toes, ten days from the 279th. (3) **The date:** Cael and Seln each know that the technician's sheet carries the day of Cael's Kindling. Neither has said or written it; neither knows what the other made of it; nobody has interpreted the sequence. No figures are ever printed, no span of years is computed, and there is **no second calendar or offset** (one registry reckoning only). Vastin does no date arithmetic and knows nothing of it. (4) **The records at the spring:** Karis has matched FOUR sign-groups to the hide and has given NO number of places; no fifth place has been described. The same stroke and bar stands in a ruled square on all four matched places' sheets (in the field: stake thirty-two and the fourth site's thirty-fours only). The remark "Behaves as device. Cannot be." is on the Stair's sheet in the fourth row; its hand is NOT identified. The instruments column reads true on every line. (5) **Seln has taught one thing, to Vastin** ("Draft nothing. Just look."). Cleon has been named (by Karis) and nothing more is known of him than her protected lines give. (6) **Oryn** went north from the fork on the 281st with the brown mule, her linen and her crossbow with one bolt; Millrace in three weeks; she owes Cael the reading ("the one thing I'm walking up that road owing"); the station term is written in the log. Her past as told at the fork: a house for the sick in a city inside the Line, the column for the patient's tier, a woman from the river sheds mended off the sheet; she has not been through a gate since. (7) **Loads:** boxes on Brom (good shoulder), Lira (both arms) and Cael; the tarred kit case on Vastin, taken up unasked at the fork-stone; Karis the flat and the hide, with the copied page in oilcloth in her coat. **Cael laid his hand on that page once, on the rise.** Vastin has walked beside Cael since the rise. (8) **Stillhounds:** a den that has learned a road runs to where a declaration ends (Karis's sixth line). The sixty yards of four-strand rope are still owed to the Lowmarch ferryman.

> **[Coordinator rulings after the Movement 4 audit, 2026-10-11 — binding; they override any plan text below that differs.]** (1) **Who is where on the 284th:** inside the iron door are Cael, Brom, Lira, Vastin and Karis, with three boxes, the tarred kit case, the flat and the hide; Cleon let them in, counted loads and not people, and shut the door. The unnamed yard clerk is outside; **Seln is above ground, NOT on the card or the count**, watching who comes to the door. Oryn is two gates north with the brown mule. (2) **The count:** Karis is the card's unnamed bearer; Cael, Brom, Lira and Vastin are the four hands, entered by count; the clerk asked nothing and wrote *four*. None of the four restricted names has been written anywhere; the asset-restriction is still pending. Cards are good only for the day written on them and are torn at each gate; Iron passes the second wall and Bronze the third by right, on a certificate that is read out and written. The way to the registry's yard is the load lane under the third wall and a cut of **twenty-two steps in five flights, a stride and a half wide, walled three men high with no door or window**; it never crosses the square. Cael has not set foot on the square or its steps and has not looked at the crown; Lira keeps the same discipline unasked. (3) **Cleon's terms** (given at the cook-house on the 283rd): all the sheets, in their places; they come down to him; no names (his is on the card and is never said aloud by him); **nobody tells him what the sheets are or how old until he has looked**; one lamp goes down with them, his, held where he says; no other carried light and no fire of anybody's. He has seen one drawn sheet and knows there are three places and fifty-two sheets, older than what the Compact found; he has not noticed Cael. **His Path has not been shown.** "Rune Path. Bronze." is all that is known of it, from Seln. (4) **Cael:** ten fragments; deployment thirty-two; the fourth and fifth self-readings were made on the 281st, round the still place and not in, uncounted; Tide feels only what flows and returned nothing ("a cord on a stone… *no length*"); he needs an instrument for what does not move, and owes Oryn the telling at the mill. Lira has read the log. (5) **Vastin and Cael:** Vastin has told him why he declined the second reading (the Ostrand case, causal; and a private relief at never learning what a plate would say); Cael has told him that the place was read twice and returned nothing, and nothing of what reads. Ground, not friendship. Vastin carries three sheets under one seal that he will not show; he has sat three times; his right knee is day five of thirty on the 284th (stick; no kneeling, running or wall) and he caught a slip on the steps with his left hand. Brom's two toes: five days left on the 284th. Lira's arm: day nineteen out of wool; her registry paper unshown; the ladder did not pull. (6) **The inn:** the long loft over the stable, by the yard half-door (the painted door has a book), for a week from the 282nd, pot at dusk, seven bowls; **purse 136 marks**. The outer gate counts feet on a bead-frame and writes no name. (7) **Stone:** the grey bridge, the registry's three lowest courses, the undercroft's end and the iron door's frame are one dark dry-laid stone older than the walls; Karis has seen it and stated no conclusion beyond "the bridge was first". The iron door has no lock or handle outside; the clerk knocks. (8) The sixty yards of four-strand were priced at the rope-walk and are to be bought going out.

> **[Coordinator rulings after the Movement 5 audit, 2026-10-11 — binding; they override any plan text below that differs.]** (1) **Eleven fragments; deployment since the Line thirty-two; purse 134 marks** at the end of Movement 5 (the night of the 290th). The eleventh, Iron-Wall-adjacent, came from Vastin in the alley's quiet minute; it did not fill, it *set*; its prompt is *This far?*; it has not been deployed and **Vastin has not been told** (Cael holds that as a debt he cannot yet pay; Lira has read the log). (2) **How acquisition lines are printed in this book:** the protected "Fragment eleven" and "Fragment twelve" units are CAEL'S OWN cataloguing of what he reads — set once, in italics, framed as his plain words; they are NOT printed as a system block and take no FRAGMENT ACQUIRED heading or code fence. (3) **The Deepstacks:** an iron door with a sliding bar inside; twenty-seven steps in three flights of nine; three galleries. Middle gallery about 110 strides, Cleon's sloped desk and fixed lamp at its end, a shelf above it with a jar, a tied bundle ("Paper. Not stone.") and the black oak plank's sacking; the fifty-two sheets on three tables, 11/29/12; the plank stands against the third table. Eastern gallery: the old town's tall tally-books. One CARRIED lamp, Cleon's, held "a hand to the left" (Cael holds it and has begun to put it where the eyes are about to go). The boxes go up at the clerk's bell and come down each morning on a fresh card; the clerk writes *The same* and *four*. (4) **Cleon** knows Karis's name (he heard Brom say it and has used it once); his own is on the card and has never been said aloud. He has shown his Path from OUTSIDE only (bear / carried / nothing; "They're orders."; "Same make."; the limit: it does not work on a river, a man or a tree, and costs his eyes for an hour). **His week runs from the 287th to the 293rd.** He has not noticed Cael and knows nothing of the alley or the mechanism. (5) **Karis** has fourteen of twenty-three letters and reads numbers and number-words only; seven kinds bear, twenty-three are carried, eleven are nothing; the bundle is tied and unread; Vastin's copied page is in her coat, unshown to Cleon. Her LEFT palm has seven stitches of Brom's under a strip of his shirt; she writes with the right. (6) **Vastin:** his right knee went again in the alley on the eleventh day of Oryn's thirty (a wall on it against her order, which he heard and asks that she be told); re-splinted with a slat from Brom's box, twice its size; on the night of the 290th he cannot stand or hold a bowl. He gave his name, rank and office aloud at the yard hatch on the 285th; nothing of it was written. He still knows nothing of what Cael carries. (7) **Brom:** the left (good) shoulder wrenched under a dray on the 286th and worse after the alley; he carries his box on the bound right shoulder, a debt entered for the mill's healers; the broker's offer (a guard's post inside the second wall) was answered "I'll think on it." and declined in fact. Lira: day twenty-five out of wool on the 290th; a bruised right shoulder; she drops from heights without the arm. (8) **Seln:** a registry clerk knows his face and voice (the price of a truthful answer at the hatch); he watches from the roof above the cut. His case is **SHUT** — never "unopened" (this edition's Book 6 opened it for the five) — and its contents are named nowhere in this book. He has seen one watcher three times (horseman's boots with the spur-rests cut away). (9) **The faction** (six, hooded, no faces, no Path opened) knows the loft and has shown that it knows; it saw Vastin's wall, a rock, a knife and a boy who stood still, and nothing of quiet ground or the fragments. Cael has ruled that the cut is not climbed at dusk again. The loft is paid to the 295th.

>
> Packet 006 carries the 291st, borrowed handcart, Karis's bound left hand, eleven fragments, and the fact that three clerk questions remain unasked (`MOVEMENT-006.md:26–28`). Its author must additionally inherit the following surviving particulars:
>
> - **Deepstacks geography and custody.** Inside the iron door: black sliding bar; twenty-seven dark-stone steps in three flights; a short cross-way; three side-by-side galleries. Middle gallery: roughly 110 strides, shelving to the roof, old sorting tables from ninety strides, Cleon's sloped desk and fixed lamp at the end, with cup/jar/brown-taped bundle/board shelf above. Eastern gallery: tall old-town ledgers and ladders. Western gallery: quarry material, where the board was found. Fifty-two first copies remain on three tables; the board is against the third and the bundle remains tied on the shelf (`chapter-27.md:5–121`; `chapter-30.md:263–273`; `chapter-33.md:305`).

She knows seven kinds bear, twenty-three are carried and eleven are ornament/nothing, that a bearer heads a run, and that board and floors repeat the same rows. She has covered one tempting six-sign group and has not guessed it. Vastin's copied page remains in her coat, unshown to Cleon (`chapter-31.md:63–189,319–339`; `chapter-32.md:3–19`; `AUTHOR-REPORT.md:111`).

His week runs from the 287th through the 293rd. He has not noticed Cael, learned of the alley, shown the inside/lean, or acquired knowledge of the mechanism (`chapter-27.md:187–199`; `chapter-29.md:193–289`; `chapter-30.md:253–279`; `AUTHOR-REPORT.md:117`).
> - **The eleventh and its entry.** Iron-Wall-adjacent, from Vastin, partial/held/defensive/rooted, tier equivalent unknown, deployment none. Cael has read it, refused its *This far?* three times, deployed nothing, and told only the log; Lira has therefore read it. Vastin has not been told. Cael recorded both Vastin's requested wall entry and more than Vastin knows (`chapter-32.md:237–257`; `chapter-33.md:149–175,205–227,291–299`; `AUTHOR-REPORT.md:105,125`).
> - **Vastin.** Right knee, original day eleven of thirty: gone sideways again, re-lathed with one slat from Brom's box, twice its former size; on the night of the 290th he cannot stand or hold a bowl. He knowingly heard and broke Oryn's no-wall order and asks that she be told he heard. He has no office on the page, no stated age, and still knows nothing of the mechanism or eleventh (`chapter-33.md:129–145,185–227,285`; `AUTHOR-REPORT.md:115`).
> - **Karis.** Left palm has a long shallow cut and seven stitches, bound in a strip of Brom's shirt; she writes right-handed. No healer inside the gate has seen her. She has three days left of Cleon's week (`chapter-33.md:63–125,277,305`; `AUTHOR-REPORT.md:111`).
> - **Brom.** Toes are done. The older right shoulder remains bound; the left/good shoulder was wrenched under the dray and worsened by the alley slab, leaving neither arm raisable above the elbow. He carries his box on the bound right shoulder. He has effectively rejected the broker's four-marks-and-found guard post, but the factor heard only `I'll think on it.` Cael has entered the shoulders as a debt for the mill's healers (`chapter-29.md:45–147`; `chapter-31.md:265–271`; `chapter-32.md:117–137`; `AUTHOR-REPORT.md:109`).
> - **What the faction knows.** It knows the loft and has deliberately shown that knowledge. It saw Vastin's uncovered face and Iron Wall, plus a rock/knife/still boy, but no Path from another crew member, no face of its own was secured, and it saw nothing of quiet ground or Cael's fragments. Seln's finding is that it wanted a visible declaration and a carried box and avoided being entered itself (`chapter-33.md:247–289`; `AUTHOR-REPORT.md:121`).
> - **Inn, purse, count and day.** Six are in the long loft over the stable; it is paid through the 295th, with pot at dusk and seven bowls. Purse 134, reserve sealed in the third box. The below-ground daily count remains Karis as bearer plus four hands; Seln is never one of them. Night of day 290 is complete; Movement 6 opens day 291. The cut will not be climbed at dusk again, but no further route ruling has been made (`chapter-31.md:265–267`; `chapter-33.md:277–305`; `AUTHOR-REPORT.md:101–103,123`).
> - **Lira and remaining objects.** Lira is day twenty-five out of wool, has a bruised right shoulder, and the left arm took nothing. Three boxes, kit case, flat and hide are in the loft; the 291st card is there; the shortened watchman's stave is with Seln; two plain bolts remain in the cut; sixty yards of four-strand are still owed and Brom means to buy them going out (`chapter-33.md:285–307`; `AUTHOR-REPORT.md:107,123`).
> - **Seln and the case.** Seln is at the ladder head/roof watch, knows the same altered boots through three sightings, and has the better half of his cord and the stave. A Treswick clerk knows his face and voice. His case is on him and **shut**; five people know its contents from Book 6, but this movement names none. He looked at the registry/front-yard-returns-door choice and decided nothing (`chapter-30.md:3–151`; `chapter-33.md:183,247–275`; `book-06 chapter-38.md:167`; `book-06 chapter-39.md:5,15,217`; `AUTHOR-REPORT.md:113`).

The source-chapter references in the line below are retained for the coordinator's and reviewers' tools only; the author does not read them. Events come from this packet, `BOOK_MAP.md` (the plan sections, the protected-text section and the reserved-truths section) and `STATE_LEDGER.md`. Write `EVENT-LIST.md` first. Where a protected unit is quoted in the plan only in part, leave `[[TIER-B: <unit> — coordinator to supply protected text]]` and write around it. [B7-reconciled 2026-10-11: amendment 1]

- Read before drafting:
  - Source `chapter-13.md`, `chapter-14.md`, `chapter-15.md` and `chapter-16.md`. Read `chapter-15.md` twice.

  - From `BOOK_MAP.md`: §2 items 23–26; §3 A (the tail), B 5; §5 (the Vastin and Oryn cutaways); §7.1 (Rune; the instruments); **§9 (all of it)**; §10 Tier A 1–3, 10, 13, 15, 17–18 and the S13–S16 Tier C lines; **§11**; §12 C10; §13.
  - `STATE_LEDGER.md`, latest section.

## Where we enter

It is the 291st day. After the alley the working party goes by the carts' street in daylight, with the boxes on a borrowed handcart. Karis's left hand is bound in a strip of Brom's shirt. Cael has eleven fragments and the eleventh has nothing to do. The clerk has not asked three things.

## What this movement is for

This movement holds the book's reveal and the arc's second pole. The twelfth arrives at a word and carries a warning. A parcel brings the second half of a competence, and the largest instrument on the continent does a wrong thing once, for him. Then the leaf is read aloud **once**, and that night Cael takes it on his feet: not excluded but *prevented*, and he happened anyway. **This is a deliberately quiet movement, with one set piece.** The reason is that the reveal must land as a discovery on paper by a researcher, and the fire must hold. The physical pressure is the tail chase and the faction's presence in the streets.

Consequential turns and promises:
- **The reader (291st).** Cleon shows the Path from inside: "A record sits … An instruction *leans* … This is a stair." Karis: "The strata lean too." Then **the acquisition at "there"**. The fifth row leans, and so does the board, and Cael does not turn the twelfth on anything else. He tells Lira first on the stair ("Twelfth. His. Lean."), then goes back down and tells Cleon at the bell, in the order he has it. "You did what." "That's not in the classification system. That's not *supposed* to be possible. That's —" / "No. I'll not say that one." The cup of water, and **the warning, verbatim**. "He went back to the carving." Vastin and Cael carry the case up the stair together. **The notice on the stair, verbatim**, with *He looked away. Kept.* The eyes are paid on the last flight. **"Cael did not tell anyone. He remembered."**
- **Rune practice (~2,000, folded across the days).** The twelfth leans at every made thing: the inn table's joints, the bridge's arches, the dividers' hinge. He learns not to look, as he learned not to look at the light. It is never used on a person.
- **The tail (291st–292nd night; ~2,500 set piece).** Seln and Cael follow a hood through the outer district. The hood goes through a tier gate Cael cannot legally cross, and he stops at the line. No faces, none taken, nothing learned but the faction's patience.
- **Dividers (292nd).** The parcel and **Hesk's note, verbatim**. The meal: four marks, two cups kept, and Seln's slip read and burned ("Closed"), leaving 130. Vastin and the cups: "Out here you count who's owed a seat." This is **the first inventory written inside walls** (§12 C10). **The light, not steady for a count of one:** "an instrument turning toward him." It is the fourth entry on the page. He does not cross. The promise holds at its first cost.
- **The leaf (293rd morning).** Describe the object before the reading: horn-coloured, point-written, the scraped name with its first upright stroke. Karis reads it **aloud once** (§9; §10 Tier A 1), then paraphrases it once: "the Architect" is a title, about four hundred years ago, and the purpose is the un-bound. "Older. Made. Us." Cael catalogues the room, not himself. **The chronicle** is marked: "Marked." / "Marked." Vastin reaches ("the mark that is given at the kindling —") and Karis answers with **two pages** and the ruler rule. The copy, with the gap drawn.
- **The yard fire (293rd evening).** Seln is told on the street ("Made." "For a kind."). Cleon comes up, the only time. Cael gives **Lira the page to read first**; nobody explains it to her. "So the whole time they weren't afraid you'd break something. They were afraid you'd *exist*." Brom prices it. Seln will keep the cache "a while longer". Karis says two pages, then sleeps on a bucket. Brom to Cleon: "Good." Vastin: "aimed … At a category. Which turned out to be a person. Who's sitting across the fire." Lira: "You signed the mark at kindlings." / "I would have." **Pole two**, taken on his feet and not catalogued, verbatim. "I want to stand in the middle of it." Cleon leaves: "Tomorrow the rest of the leaf. The list of parts."

**Physical challenge:** the tail (set piece). The eyes' cost on the stair.
**Cutaways (~4,500):** Vastin, ~2,000, alone after the leaf. "Aimed." His sentence stays stopped in his head too. **He does not join the leaf to the sigils, does no date arithmetic, and names no seals.** Oryn, ~2,500, at a hold on her route, where a fourteen-year-old's Kindling happens. She watches a child see a sigil nobody else can see, and thinks of a dark one. **She has not heard the leaf. Nobody dies.**
**Development:** about 16 Cael beats (the middle-third bump). Supporting: Lira 2, Karis 2, Cleon 2, Vastin 2, Seln 1–2, Brom 1, Oryn 1.

## What must remain true / withheld

- **The reveal is spoken once.** "Four hundred years" appears only in the record's voice, in Karis's single paraphrase, and in Cael's pole-two sentence. "The Architect" is a title, never an agent.
- **There is no line between the leaf and the sigils**, anywhere, in any head. Never use *terminal*, *circuit* or *infrastructure*.
- The chronicle's hole stays a hole. Book 11's plant is marked, not read.
- The twelfth is not turned on Cael's own still place in this movement. It does not turn unbidden until Millrace (Movement 7).
- The light is deniable. Nothing hints at agency. Nineteen, and **no birthday**. "Eight days inside" is counted from the 284th.
- Cleon looks away from the boy on purpose and keeps that, and says no more than the leaf says. Karis does not theorize. The record's fear is reported, never endorsed.

## What the author owns

The chamber's light on the leaf, and the length of the silence before Karis reads. The texture of the meal. The route of the tail. How Lira reads the page. The pacing of the fire's voices within the fixed order: Lira first, then the circle, then Vastin, then Cael.

## Where we leave pressure

"Now what." There is a bearing, a set of marks nobody can read yet, and a site at the end of the line that has been quiet since they built the machine. He wants to stand in the middle of it. Tomorrow comes the list of parts, then the road east.

---

## PRECEDING CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-08-before-the-paths/manuscript/chapter-33.md

# Chapter 33 — Entered

They let the old man down onto the stair between them, and for a while nobody did anything but breathe.

He sat on the seventh step with his back against the wall and the bound leg thrust out across the slot. He had not fainted. His eyes were open, but they were looking at a strip of sky, and the hands that lay in his lap had fallen open like a pair of gloves. The stick had gone down two flights by itself. Lira fetched it, and laid it across his knees, and he did not take hold of it.

Cael had seen that man at the floor once, on a moor, where a healer had said so with two fingers on his wrist and had likened it to a well. He had bent over his stick then, and stood. He could not have stood now if the stair had been on fire.

"Who's hurt?" said Cael. "Say it. All of you."

"Shoulder," said Lira. "I hit the wall with it. It wasn't the arm, and I want that written; I never put the arm to anything."

"The left," said Brom. "It's worse than the cart left it. And I've had the right round the pair of you, so that's said something to me as well." He lowered himself onto his box. "I'll be hearing the rest of it from two healers at once."

Karis did not answer. She was still on the sixth step, where she had sat down, with the flat across her knees and her left hand inside her coat.

"Karis."

"It is my hand," she said, and brought it out.

She had not had her gloves on. They were in her pocket, where they had been since the forenoon, since a person does not turn the leaves of a tally-book in kid. The stone's edge had gone across the heel of her palm, from the root of the thumb to the wrist's other side, as straight as a line ruled with her own brass; and she had it shut upon a fold of her skirt, and there was a good deal of red in the fold.

"It is not deep. I looked. It is only long."

Brom had his neckcloth off already. He wound it round the hand, twice, and tied it, and she let him.

So that was the count, and Cael went over it as he would have gone over a cut on a moor. There had been six of them, with staves and a bow, in a place with no way out of it; and nobody was dead, and nobody had been carried off. One box had been dragged down two steps, and sat upon. Not one of the six had shown so much as a chin.

They had left a stave. It lay where Lira had dropped it, with her knife standing up out of the middle; and she put her boot on the ash and worked the blade free with both hands, the right and then, slowly, the left.

"That's good steel," she said. "It was dull for half a year. I'd not have got a finger's depth with it before the smith had it."

"Leave the stave," said a mild voice above them. "I shall want to look at it."

Seln was at the head of the cut. He came down the flights as he came down a ladder, winding a thin cord round his fingers as he came, with a small pear of lead swinging from its end. It was a thing off a desk: a copying clerk hangs such a weight over the edge of a ledger, to keep his page open.

"The four went by the lane," he said, "two east and two west, at a walk, and they had their hoods back before the first corner. I did not see a face. The two below went out by the carts' gate, which had been left upon the latch for them." He stopped a step above the old man, and looked at him for some time. "I could take one of the four, and I took the one who was there to be believed."

"They cut your cord," said Lira.

"They did. I have the better half of it."

He put it into his sleeve. Then he went down on his heels, which Cael had seldom seen him do for any person, and got his shoulder under a brown coat's left arm.

"He cannot stay in a stair. They will count you out at two gates, and it is past the bell already. I can bring him to the head of this, and no farther; I am on no card."

They took him up between them, Seln and Cael, a step at a time. The sound leg did what it could, and the other hung. At each of the thirteen steps to the lane the old man let his breath out through his nose, and said nothing at all.

At the top Seln slid out from under the arm, and Brom came into his place with the wrong shoulder.

"I shall be at the arch before you," said Seln; and he went along the parapet, and was not there.

It was full dusk in the lane. The four alleys had no white in them. Each had, at its far end, the paleness that did not flicker, and it lay out along the cobbles in a bar. Cael went through all four with an old man's arm across his neck and a box dragging at his back, and he did not turn his head. It was not hard, tonight. He had too much on his shoulders to look at anything but the next sett; and a girl went by on his left each time, under two loads, with a tarred case knocking at her hip.

"You're past the bell," said the old keeper at the round arch. He had his board on his knee, and a lantern beside the stool, and the point of his pen went along them. "One, and four. Your lame one is a good deal lamer."

"He fell on your steps," said Karis. She had her left hand inside her coat. It was true, and she said it to the wall.

"I told you to mind him on them; they are older than he is." He made his stroke. "Go on. I shall not write the lateness against the card, this once, since I should not care to explain that card to anybody."

---

There was a house inside the second wall with a board at its door, and Lira spoke of it before the lantern was lit.

"It's two streets from the blue gate. We've gone by it a dozen times. *Second bell: blue.* She's Iron, the third rank, and she's got it on paper." She had Karis's wrist in her right hand, and was holding the whole arm up above the pallet, as a healer had once told her to hold her own. "They'd have that shut in a quarter of an hour, with a Path. It'd not even leave a mark."

"They would," said Seln. He was on the ladder's head, where he had been when they came in under the arch. "It would be the best-mended hand in the district. And before any healer in that house laid a finger on it, a clerk would write a line on a sheet at a bed's foot: her name, and where the name is registered, and the tier; and the sheet goes to the counter at the first bell."

"It is the ninth article of the schedule," said a voice from the gable, very faintly. "The entry precedes the treatment. I have signed for the keeping of it."

They had laid the old man on the pallet under the blanketed window. He had not spoken since the cut, and Cael had thought him asleep.

"After the fourth bell there's the side door," said Lira. "With a card. It said so."

"*As the house's means allow.* A card is a figure, and a figure is seen when those with names have been seen. She would sit on that bench until the morning; and in the morning they would still ask her what she was."

"I am not going to be written down for a hand," said Karis.

She said it quite calmly. She was as white as the wall, and she sat very upright, with her hurt hand held up for her and the other flat on a courier's flat.

"I have three days, and I have fourteen letters. If a paper is served on me tomorrow, at a registry's own yard door, then four people have carried fifty-two sheets up that hill for a week to no purpose, and a man below will never be told why I did not come down." She drew a breath. "It is my left. I write with the other."

There was no healer on that side of a gate who would put hands on her for less than her name. The one who would have done it for a bowl of barley was five gates north, with a mule; and she would have asked first, and been told yes, and been grey for an hour afterward.

"Then it's me," said Brom.

He had got up off his box. He felt under its lashing with his right hand, and brought out a twist of cloth; and in the cloth there was a needle, as long as a finger and a little curved, and a hank of thread that had been drawn through wax. He had roped three boxes with those, at a ferry's shed and in an inn yard.

Karis looked at the needle.

"You have sewn a person before."

"A dog. And a boot. And the lining of her coat, on a mountain."

"I am to take comfort from the coat?"

"The dog lived," said Brom. "It lived to bite the carter."

It was the last thing that anybody said to make another smile. He held the needle in the lantern's flame, turning it, until the steel had gone dull, as he had seen a smith do for a horse; and then he sat down on the boards, since he could not lift either arm to a table's height, and laid her hand palm upward upon his own knee.

"Lira. The light. Lower than that; I want it where I'm looking, and not in my eyes."

"A hand to the left," said Lira, and knelt.

She held the lantern in her right hand, with her elbow on her thigh, and it did not move at all.

Brom took the neckcloth off. He looked for a long moment, with his head on one side, as he had looked at a pier and at a heap of slate; and then he began at the thumb's end.

He was slow. He had hands like shovels, and no arm behind either of them, and he worked from the wrists. He drew each stitch down flat and looked at it before he made the next. Karis did not watch. She had turned her face to the slates, and her lips were moving; and it was a little while before Cael understood what she was saying over.

It was the letters. She was telling them off, the hooked upright and the gate and the fork, each with its sound, one to a stitch.

There were seven stitches. She had come to the seventh letter by then, and she went on through the other seven with her eyes shut while he tied off.

He had no hand free for a knife, and so he bent his head over her palm, and bit the thread through.

"That's my whole stock of sewing," he said. "I've put it all in the one place."

He tore a strip from the tail of his shirt, the width of three fingers, and bound the hand from the knuckles to the wrist; and he tucked the end in, and pressed it flat with his thumb, as he pressed a knot that he meant to trust.

"I looked which hand it was, before I started," he said. "If it had been the one that draws, I'd not have touched it. I'd have carried you up to that board myself, and given them my own name to write beside yours."

Karis opened her eyes. She turned the bound hand over, and looked at the back of it, and then at him.

"Thank you," she said. "I know what that would have cost you, and I am very glad that it was the left."

---

Vastin could not hold a bowl.

Brom had brought the pot up, late, with seven on the board, and had set the seventh at the end by itself. The old man had been got up against the gable's foot, with a rolled coat behind him; and when he put out his right hand for the thing, the fingers would not shut upon its rim. The left, which had held a ferry's strap and a mule's head, lay open on the straw and did not stir.

"I am aware," he said, "that I am about to be fed like a horse."

"You're about to be fed like him," said Lira, with a jerk of her chin at Cael. "He couldn't lift a spoon for a day, after a stair. I did that as well."

She held the bowl to the old man's mouth, and he drank from its edge. He did it with his eyes on the far wall, and he thanked her after every mouthful, until she told him that she would stop if he did it again.

Brom had the linen off the knee. One of the two laths had cracked along its length. He took a slat from the lid of his own box, and split it with Lira's knife, and bound the joint again as he had seen a healer do it, turn upon turn.

"It's twice the size that it was," he said. "I can't tell you what's in it. I can only make it so that it won't bend."

"I was told what it would cost," said Vastin. "She was exact, and I should like it remembered that she was."

Nobody answered him. Cael had heard those words given, on a frozen road, by a square woman with her sleeves pushed up: that a man who stood a wall on that leg inside the month would keep the stick for the rest of his days. It was the eleventh day of thirty.

He went and sat down on his own pallet, at last, with his back against the third box.

It had been waiting for him since the stair. He had felt it there through all that came after, through a lane and two gates and seven stitches, standing in the place behind his eyes that belonged to nothing else, as each of the ten had stood, as patient as a clerk. He had not looked. He had had an old man's arm across his neck.

He looked at it now, in a loft that smelled of hay and horse and a pot of barley.

The lines were where they had always been, and nobody under those slates could see them but himself. He took them as he had taken the last, on a healer's plank: slowly, from the top, putting each into the plain words in which he would have to write it.

*Iron-Wall-adjacent. Vastin. Partial. Iron family. Held. A plane, not a body: the ground told not to give. Distinct from the third — Iron-adjacent, the bed; this is not the bed hardened, this is a line drawn on it and kept. Engagement field: defensive; rooted. Tier equivalent unknown. Deployment: none. Eleventh.*

Eleven.

He went over what was new in it, since he could not have helped doing so.

It was of a family that he had already. The third had come to him from a big man, a long while ago, and it read the ground under his feet; and he had half expected this to be the same thing made harder. It was not. The millstone told him what a floor weighed. This told a floor where it was to stop.

It had a word in it that he had not met, and the word was *held*. The ten had each been named for what they did, since they read, and burst, and burned, and bound; this one was named for what it did not do.

Its field was a new one as well. He had nine that had come out of a fight, and their fields said so. He had one that a healer had given him, lying down, and that said *clinical*. Here was a thing that had come out of a fight in which the person who owned it had struck nobody. A man had put his heels on a line and been hit, and the lines had found a name for that, and it was two words long.

And it had no tier. Every one of the others had been weighed, Iron or Bronze or heavier; but an old man had told a factor on a moor that this Path had a rank beside it on a roll which would mean nothing on that road, and it seemed that it meant nothing here.

He had it, then. It sat under his breastbone with the rest, as real as any of them, and as quiet as a gatepost in a field at night.

*This far?* it said, of the ladder's head; and of the boards between his boots; and of the door of the stable underneath.

He told it no, and thought that he would be telling it so for a long while.

There was one thing more, and he had kept it for the last, as a person keeps the worst line of a bill. He knew whose it was. He had not had to grieve for a name that he would never learn, as he had once on a chalk bank; the name was three yards from him, against a gable, being given water by a girl, and it could not close its hand upon a bowl.

---

"I should be obliged for a word," said Vastin. "I regret that I cannot come to you for it."

The lantern had been turned low. Brom was down, on his back, and Karis lay by the boxes with her bound hand on her chest. Lira had gone to her own pallet; and Seln sat on the ladder's head, where he could hear the yard, and might have heard anything else besides.

Cael went and sat on the boards beside the old man's straw.

"I have been setting it in order," said Vastin. He spoke to the slates, and his voice had very little breath behind it. "It is a habit, and I am told that it is the tell; I shall give it up when I am stronger. There are three heads."

"Say them."

"The first is the knee. I was forbidden a wall on it, by the one person on this side of the Line who had the right to forbid me, and I stood one. I did not forget her; I had her in my mind as the stick came down, word for word." He shut his eyes. "I do not ask that it be excused. I ask only that she be told that I heard her."

"I'll tell her. She'll have a good deal to say about it."

"I should think the less of her if she had not."

"The second is what they came for. I have stood at the foot of eleven fields, and I know when a crowd wants a door and when it wants a man, and those six wanted neither. They had a light, and they kept it dark, and they looked past me. I stood uncovered, a yard from one of them, with a face that forty years of clerks have been obliged to know; and he did not once look at it." Something moved at the corner of the old mouth. "I find that I was a little affronted. They were looking at you."

Cael did not answer.

"I do not know what they hoped to see, and I do not ask. I told you on a bank that I had measured a person and could not read him, and it is as true tonight. I sat four feet from you for three days, with a plate, and I have walked a shoulder off you since a ferry. I know that six people shut a stair to watch you do something, and that you went up a step into a crossbow's eye and did nothing whatever." He drew what breath he had. "I know no more of what you are than the brass did. That is the third head, and it is the one that I wished to say."

He opened his eyes, and turned them, for the first time, to Cael's face.

"I put the wall in front of it anyway. I'd like that entered somewhere, since I'm not the one who keeps the ledger any more."

"It's entered," Cael said.

"Thank you."

He had said it so on a bank, with a hand on a boy's arm; he had no hand to put there tonight.

Cael sat on.

It was entered. He had not lied to the man by the width of a hair, since it was written already in the only place where such a thing was ever written first; and there was more in the entry than the man had asked for, or could have guessed.

He had taken something from him. He had not reached. It had come as nine of the ten had come, out of standing within a stride of a person who was using what he had in earnest. It had come while that person poured himself into a line on a step, to keep a bolt off a face that he could not read. Cael had stood there and been given it.

He could tell him. He looked at that squarely, as he had looked at a worse thing on a healer's plank.

He had told the healer within the minute, with her palm still on him. But she had known already what he was; she had found the ten with her own two hands, and it had been hers to be told.

This man knew nothing of it. He had not asked on a bank what read a still place, and he had not asked tonight what six people came to see. To say *I have your wall in me* was to say how it had got there, and how it got there was the one thing that Cael did not own alone. Five others held it. They had kept it out of every book for longer than he could say, and they had kept it above all from the office that lay on that straw with its hands open.

He believed that the old man would have done nothing with it. That was not the question, since it was not his to spend upon a belief.

So it was a debt, and of a kind that he had not carried before. He had owed a healer a telling, and had paid it. He owed this one, and could not pay; and the man had thanked him.

He got up, and went back to his pallet, and took Hesk's book out of his coat.

Lira was not asleep. She had turned on her right side, with the slow arm uppermost, and her eyes were open in the lantern's last light.

"It's in there, then," she said, hardly above a breath. "Whatever it is that you've had on your face since the stair."

"It will be. Give me a quarter of an hour."

"Does he know?"

"No."

She looked across the loft, at a long shape under a blanketed window.

"That's a lot to owe a man who can't hold a cup," said Lira.

---

Seln came down off the ladder's head before the pencil was wet.

"I have a finding," he said. "I have looked at it by a lantern, and I shall look again by day; but I do not think that it will alter."

He had brought the stave up under his coat, and he laid it now on the boards in the round of the light, with the cut that a long knife had made in its middle.

"It is a watchman's, shortened, and the ferrule has been filed where a maker's mark would stand. The bolts are the same: a plain shaft, and no fletcher's thread." He put his hands into his sleeves. "Six people came into a stair in the oldest town on the Line, with their faces tied up, and not one of them opened a Path. They did not lack for them. I know how the one on the landing stood."

"Why not, then?" said Lira. "There were six. They'd have gone through Brom like a door."

"Because a Path that is opened under that wall is entered by whoever sees it; and they took the same care that we did to have nothing of theirs entered. They wished to have something of ours." His eyes went to Cael, and away. "The bow was to make one of you open, and the light was to tell somebody below that it had been done. When the light could not be shown, they had no reason to stay, and they went. That is their limit: they cannot be written down in this town any more than we can."

Cael had the pencil, and he wrote it as it was said, under the day.

"And the box?" said Brom, from the dark. "One of them had his fist through my becket. He never looked at me."

"They were told to bring away what was carried. I do not think that they were told what it would look like." The mild voice did not alter; but the left hand had come out of its sleeve, and it rested for a moment on a strap. "What they would have been glad to find was on a roof above their heads, for the whole of that minute. I had not thought to be grateful to a parapet."

Nobody asked him what he meant. Five of them knew; and whether the sixth, against the gable, was awake to hear it, Cael could not have told.

"There is one thing more," said Seln. "I came down by my own way, and I was at this arch before you. There was a man on the step of the painted door, under its lamp, beside the book."

"The one in the doorway," said Cael. "With the boots."

"They were the same boots. He was not in a doorway tonight. He stood in the light, where a person stands who wishes to be seen, with his hood up; and he waited there until the five of you had come in under the arch, and then he walked away along the ring road without hurrying."

"So they know where we sleep," said Lira.

"They have known it for four nights. Tonight they have told us that they know. It is a civility, in that trade, and it is meant to be thought about."

Cael thought about it. He had six people in a loft, of whom one could not stand, and another had seven stitches in her hand; and there was not a second house under that wall which would take them without a name. There were three days of a week, and fifty-two sheets on three tables, and a small pale woman who would go down to them tomorrow if she had to be carried.

"We don't go up that stair at dusk again," he said. "Whatever else we do. I'll say the rest in the morning, when I can see it."

"That is sensible," said Seln; and he went back to the ladder's head, and sat, with his face to the yard.

Cael wrote the count first, as an old man in Denvash had taught him.

*Two hundred and ninetieth day; Treswick; the loft; a hundred and thirty-four. The cut, at dusk: six, hooded, two from the yard and four from the lane, with staves, one bow and one dark lantern, and no Path opened by any of them. Nobody dead, none taken, no faces. Karis: the left palm, on a step's edge; seven stitches, Brom's; no healer, since a healer here wants a name. Brom: both shoulders. Lira: the right shoulder, on a wall, and the arm did not take it. V.: a wall, on the eleventh day of thirty; the knee has gone again, and he cannot hold a bowl.*

*Finding, S.'s: they cannot be written here either. They wanted one of us to open, and a light shown for it, and a box; they got a wall and a rock, and they left when the light failed.*

*They know the loft, and they've said so.*

*Deployment since the Line: thirty-two.*

He ruled a line under that, and sat with the pencil over the leaf for some time. Then he wrote the rest, and it came all at once, as the largest things did.

*Eleventh. Iron Wall, from Vastin, in an alley, while he held a line for us with nothing behind him but the decision to. It's not Brom's — Brom gets harder, this holds. Since my Kindling I've learned to give ground and disappear. Now I've got the opposite in me, from the man who spent forty years being the wall they put in front of people like me. He held it for us instead. I don't have a word for what that is. I wrote it down anyway.*

Under it he put two lines more, since they were owed.

*Not told. I can't tell the taking without telling the rest, and the rest is six people's. He asked for the wall to be entered, and I said that it was.*

He turned the book round, and held it out across the gap between two pallets. Lira read it with her chin down, and she read the last two lines twice. She did not say anything at all. She shut the book over her thumb, and gave it back, and lay looking at the slates.

The lantern was nearly out. By its last brown light he could see Karis, on her back, with a strip of a big man's shirt lying white upon her chest; and she was not asleep, for her lips were moving still.

She had fourteen letters, and three days left of a week that a man below had called his own. There was a plank of black oak propped against a table in the dark, two hundred yards under her head, and a bundle on a shelf above it that nobody had untied.

Cael lay down. He put the book under the box's becket, where an awl had mended it.

*This far?* said the eleventh, of the dark.

*No*, he said. He had a lamp to hold in the morning, and he found that he knew already which way it would be wanted.

---

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — editions/monroe-1.3/EDITION_BRIEF.md

# The Fractured Path — Monroe Jackson 1.3 edition (O'Connor 1.3 seat)

Owner direction, 2026-10-01: rewrite the whole Fractured Path series through the
O'Connor 1.3 seat on the Monroe Jackson 1.3 foundation, with Claude Opus 5.5 as the
manuscript author, at about 300,000 words per book. When a book is complete, render
every chapter through Breeze and publish it as a new edition. The current edition
stays exactly as it is; this edition is new and recoverable.

Public byline: Monroe Jackson. Seat: `oconnor` 1.3.0. Foundation: Monroe Jackson 1.3.0.
Requested author: Opus (Claude Opus 5.5). Coordinator: this orchestrator session.
Review seat: Sol (gpt-5.6-sol via `codex exec`) — a different model family from the author.

## What "rewrite" means here

This is a retelling at full length, not a copy-edit. The prose is new. The story is the
same series.

**Keep (canon — binding):**
- Every book's spine: the major events, their order and outcomes, who lives and dies,
  the ending state, ages, the calendar where later books depend on it.
- Cael's fragment acquisitions — which fragment, in which book, under what engagement —
  and the counts at each book's close. The system notice texts that later books quote.
- The SECRET reveal schedule in `universe/CANON_RULES.md` and every reserved truth. A
  rewrite may plant more; it may never disclose earlier than the schedule.
- The universe rules, costs and limits in `universe/UNIVERSE_BIBLE.md`.
- Relationships and their turning points as the existing books establish them.
- Lines a later book quotes or calls back (log entries, notices, signature exchanges).
  The book planner lists these as protected wording.

**Change (the point of the edition):**
- Length: about 300,000 words per book (owner). The existing books are 100–125k. The
  extra ~190k comes from: developed fights given room (1,500–2,500+ words when the
  changing problem earns it); learning encounters and training that show effort turning
  into capability; events the current edition summarizes, put on the page; supporting
  cast given private wants, independent decisions and arcs across the formula's 8–10
  person cast; humor and warmth between people; brief POV cutaways; new episodes that
  fit canon and do not alter any book's end state.
- Shape: open-range movements of six or more chapters, one compact brief per movement,
  no per-chapter cards or per-chapter gates. Roughly 55–65 chapters of ~5,000 words per
  book in 7–9 movements; the author owns chapter boundaries.
- Rhythm: the owner-selected numerical formula (compiled into every prompt). Baseline
  of the current edition, measured by `tools/formula_metrics.py`: sentence mean ~17.5
  (target 14.6), paragraph median ~44 words (target ~18), Flesch RE ~67 (target 72.3),
  FK grade ~8.3 (target 6.8). The edition should move toward the targets.
- POV: about 87% Cael, about 13% brief purposeful cutaways spread over 4–5 named
  characters. Cutaways may never reveal what a reserved-truth boundary withholds.

## Non-negotiable

- **Reader Standard (Gate 27, owner):** written for a thirteen-year-old. Clean language
  (no profanity, obscenity, crude slang or blasphemy, including "damn", "hell" as an
  oath, "bastard" and softer cousins); moral goodness (honesty, courage, loyalty,
  restraint, care for the weak, at a cost; wrong named as wrong; cruelty never
  rewarded); violence with cost, fear and consequence and no gore; no sexual content
  or innuendo. Outranks every voice preference. See `craft/VOICE_CHARTER.md` end section.
- **Names:** keep every existing canon name. The registry's flagged collisions
  (Vell/Velmere, the -vane cluster, Wray/Greyvane, Bracken/Brom) are the owner's call
  and are NOT renamed in this edition. New minor characters the expansion needs may be
  named by the planner, screened by ear against `craft/NAME_REGISTRY.md`, and listed
  under "Names pending owner approval" in the book map. Never invent a name for an
  existing canon role.
- **Audio-first:** this ships as an audiobook. Clear referents, clear attribution,
  names distinct by ear, punctuation that exposes meaning.

## Where things live

- Canon (read-only for this edition): `universe/UNIVERSE_BIBLE.md`,
  `universe/CANON_RULES.md`, `universe/STATE_LEDGER.md`, `craft/NAME_REGISTRY.md`,
  `craft/VOICE_CHARTER.md`, `series/THE_FRACTURED_PATH_SERIES.md`.
- Source edition (the story being retold): `books/<book>/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md` and
  `books/<book>/chapters/chapter-NN.md`. Read them for events, not for sentences.
- This edition: `editions/monroe-1.3/<book>/` — `BOOK_MAP.md`, `STATE_LEDGER.md`,
  `packets/MOVEMENT-NNN.md`, `manuscript/chapter-NN.md`, `state/movement-NNN/`.
- Series-level edition maps: `editions/monroe-1.3/SERIES_MAP.md`, `CHARACTERS.md`.
- Metrics: `python3 editions/monroe-1.3/tools/formula_metrics.py <chapters...>`.

## Rhythm calibration (measured, 2026-10-01)

Book 1 Movement 1 — the first movement drafted from this brief — overshot the formula in
the short direction: sentence mean 8.8 words (target 14.6), 41% of sentences at five
words or fewer (target ~28%), 0.3% at forty-plus (target ~3.3%), Flesch-Kincaid grade 2.8
(target 6.8), about 106 dialogue tags per 10k words (target ~41). Paragraph median (15)
and scene spacing were close. So, when drafting:

- Let thought, action and description run in full, well-built sentences. Compound and
  complex sentences with a clear hierarchy are the house texture, not the exception.
- Join a run of three or four clipped statements into one sentence when they are one thought.
- Keep short sentences for beats that land — a recognition, a hit, a turn — not as default.
- Give each chapter a few deliberately long, readable sentences (forty words or more)
  where an action or a thought earns the length.
- Drop "he said" / "she said" when the paragraph already makes the speaker clear.
- Keep short paragraphs (median ~18 words) and scene breaks about every 950 words.

## No scripted prose surgery (2026-10-01)

Rhythm and length repairs are done by reading, sentence by sentence, never by a script that
splits sentences or paragraphs at clause or sentence boundaries. Two repairs did that and
each needed a recheck to find the splits that broke a thought. Where the formula and a
character's speech disagree, keep the speech: one-to-three-word dialogue lines and the short
landing beats of a fight are not drift to be repaired.

## Working ranges and accepted drift (coordinator, 2026-10-01, after five repairs)

Five movement repairs show three of the formula's numbers pulling against the brief's other
asks (full sentences as house texture, speech kept as people speak, paragraphs broken where the
thought turns). Repairs that chase one of them push another out. So, until the owner rules
otherwise, these are the working ranges a movement is held to, and repairs aim at them — not at
the decimals:

| Measure | Formula target | Working range | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Sentence mean | 14.6 | 13–15.5 | primary target — keep |
| ≥40-word share | 3.3% | 2.5–4.5% | primary target — keep |
| Words per scene | ~950 | 850–1,050 | primary target — keep |
| ≤5-word share | 27.7% | up to ~34% | dialogue-heavy movements; short speech is kept |
| Paragraph median | ~18 | up to ~30 | the 18 is an ASR pause proxy, not print; long cutaways run longer |
| Flesch-Kincaid grade | 6.8 | 3.5–6 | clear short words; the syllable estimator is approximate |

Measured drift outside a working range is reported in the movement's review; a repair is for
the primary three and for concrete defects.

## Source-reuse gate tightened to 8 words (coordinator, 2026-10-02)

The 10-word gate let distinctive source phrasing through in 6–9-word fragments (the first
movement's opening line, for example, echoed the current edition's). Measured at 8 words, movements
written after authors began self-checking run 1–6 shared runs per 10k; the worst ran ~30. From now
on `ed.sh overlap` and every editorial review use runs of **8 or more** words. Every listed run that
BOOK_MAP does not protect is rewritten — re-composed, not a synonym swapped. Names, Path names,
ranks and plain facts of canon will sometimes share a short run; re-compose the sentence around them.
Movements closed under the 10-word gate get one book-level 8-word sweep at book completion.

## Draft from your own event list, not from the source page (coordinator, 2026-10-02)

Book 3 Movement 6's review found the retold scenes in ch42–44 to be close paraphrase: 23–32% of
sentences followed a source sentence word for word in order, with words varied enough to pass the
8-word gate. Everything the author invented was clean. The cause is drafting with the source open.
So, for every movement:

1. Read the source chapters the packet names, once, for what happens and who these people are.
2. Close them. Write a private event list in your own words — what happens, in what order, who
   wants what, what changes. Keep it in your working notes, not in the manuscript.
3. Draft from that list and the book map. Do not reopen the source chapters while drafting.
   Choose your own entry point into each scene and your own order of beats; the source's scene
   shape is not canon, only its events are.
4. Only protected wording (BOOK_MAP) is copied, exactly, from the map — not from the source page.

---

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — editions/monroe-1.3/book-08-before-the-paths/BOOK_MAP.md

# BOOK MAP — The Fractured Path, Book 8: Before the Paths (Monroe Jackson 1.3 edition)

Seat: O'Connor 1.3.0 (`oconnor`), foundation Monroe Jackson 1.3.0. Public byline: Monroe Jackson.
Planning seat model: Claude Opus 5.5 (`claude-opus-5-5`), as reported by the runtime. Requested manuscript author: Opus.
Plan date: 2026-10-01. Status: PLAN ONLY. No manuscript prose exists for this edition.

Historical planning sources: `books/book-08-before-the-paths/chapters/chapter-01.md` … `chapter-24.md` and the associated architecture/rulings. Those source chapters were used by the planner and are reviewer-only under the no-source method. Monroe 1.3 canon is the edition manuscript and CLOSED ledgers. Book 8 entry canon is the CLOSED Book 7 manuscript, especially edition chapters 45–60, and Book 7 `STATE_LEDGER.md` AFTER MOVEMENT 7 and 8. The seam forward remains the series bible's Book 9 entry (§15). [B7-reconciled 2026-10-11: amendment 3]

**Shape at a glance:** 9 movements · 60 chapters · 300,000 words · average 5,000 words per chapter · largest movement 36,000 words.

**Book promise (series bible):** make the reader feel the weight of history: the present is built on something that was deliberately buried.
**Internal arc:** *the system was built to exclude me* → *the system was built specifically to prevent me* → at the oldest centre, *he fit*. The third sentence past those two stays unwritten.
**The title:** "Before the Paths" names two things. It is the era before the classification existed, when the un-bound walked in the land. It is also the book's argument: everything in Valdris was laid on top of a buried thing by someone who wanted it to stay buried. The book's image is a Compact registry built on the roof of a pre-Compact quarry it has never read.

---
